========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... I mean, I usually do check whatever I'm doing, because it sounds so real. It's not like I think it's some magic spell forcing me to do it or anything like that, it's just... it's so clear. Clear as day. Sometimes when I'm in the aisle at work—like down in the shipping section where it's usually super loud with the forklifts backing up and the warning beepers going off—I'll just be wrapping a pallet or checking a packing slip and I hear this man's voice right over my shoulder. "Nicole, check it again." 

And I always turn around, thinking it's Greg messing with me, or maybe the shift supervisor coming up behind me to check my tags, but there's nobody there. Nobody is near me at all, just empty concrete and racks of boxes. 

And it's not even just at the warehouse where it's busy and there's people around. It happens when it's dead quiet, too. Like, I'll be at home folding clothes in the living room. Just standing there sorting my husband's work shirts—the heavy denim ones that I always have to double-check the collars on for grease stains before I put them on the hangers—and matching up all his black socks, and I'll hear the exact same man's voice. "Nicole, check it again." Clear as day, right in my own house. 

So, yeah, I guess I do follow the instruction, because I'll stop and re-check the collar on the shirt, or I'll recount the pairs of socks in the basket, because the voice is just so... present, you know? It's not a whisper. It doesn't sound like it's in my head. It's just a guy telling me to check my work. So I do. I mean, if someone was standing right next to you and told you to check something, you'd probably look at it again too, right? But I'll look around the living room, and my husband is out in the driveway working on his truck, the TV isn't even on yet, and I'm completely alone. But I still hear it. I just check the clothes again and keep folding.

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm only sitting in this chair today because my husband just wouldn't drop it. Him and all the people down at the warehouse where I work, they just keep acting like I'm crazy. Like I'm making things up out of thin air or losing my mind. But I'm not stupid, you know? I might not have finished high school, but I know how to do my job. I move freight and load pallets all day, I bust my back for them, and I know for an absolute fact they have been watching my every single move on the floor. \n\nIt’s all because that new supervisor spot opened up. I’ve been working my tail off trying to prove I deserve it, and now suddenly all my numbers are getting messed up. I'll wrap and move a whole section, I'll count them myself, and then I find out they are going in behind my back and changing the pallet counts in the system. I know they are. The shift report comes out and it's completely different from what I actually moved. They're doing it on purpose, teaming up to sabotage my numbers just to keep me from getting that supervisor spot. And when I try to explain this to my husband, how they're whispering by the loading docks and messing with my logs, he just looks at me like I'm the one who's unhinged and tells me I need to get my head checked. So here I am, but I'm telling you, I'm not the one with the problem here.\n\nDoctor: If you saw the actual computer logs and records showing that your pallet counts were correct and hadn't been changed, would that convince you, or do you think they might have altered those records too?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing about the computers they have down there. You have to understand how the system works. It's not just a matter of looking at a screen and saying, 'Oh, there's the number.' The shift managers, they have override codes. I've seen them use them when someone messes up a barcode scan. So if you showed me the logs, I mean, what does that even prove? They could just go into the system—because they all have those little tablets they carry around on the floor, the ones with the blue cases—and they can just type in whatever they want before the final report even gets printed out. \n\nAnd it's not just the computers, it's the whole way they're acting. Like yesterday, I was loading up the west bay, doing my job, sweating through my shirt, and I saw two of them. So even if the computer says one thing, I know what I'm seeing with my own two eyes. They're watching every single thing I do. They want that supervisor job for one of their buddies, probably Mark since they're always joking around together, so they're changing my numbers to make me look slow. My husband says, 'Oh, you're just stressed, you're just paranoid,' but he doesn't work there! He doesn't see how they operate. He just wants me to come here and let you tell me I'm crazy, but I'm not. I'm just telling you what's actually happening to me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned seeing Greg and Mark staring and muttering at you by the breakroom. Other than watching you and changing your numbers, what else do you think they might be doing or planning against you?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I think they’re trying to build a whole case against me, like a whole file or something, just to get me fired, not just passed over. Because, like I said, I only came in here because my husband and the warehouse people keep acting like I'm the one who's crazy, but I *know* they have been watching my moves and changing the pallet counts to keep me from getting that supervisor spot. And it's not just the numbers. I bet they're messing with the equipment I use, too. \n\nLike, the other day, I went to grab my usual pallet jack—the yellow one, the one with the squeaky left wheel that I always use because it turns better in the tight aisles—and it was just gone. Nowhere in the bay. I had to spend ten minutes looking for it, and when I finally found it all the way over in receiving, the handle was jammed. I know Greg did that. He probably walked it all the way over there while I was on my fifteen-minute break. They want me to look incompetent. They want the big boss, Mr. Henderson, to walk the floor and see me struggling with a jammed jack or looking for my equipment, so he thinks, 'Oh, she can't handle the pressure, she's too disorganized.' \n\nAnd my husband, he just sighs and says, 'Maybe someone just borrowed it.' Borrowed it? In a warehouse with fifty other jacks? No, they took *mine* because they know my routine. They’re studying me. They watch when I take my breaks, they watch how fast I wrap a skid, they watch everything. They’re trying to make me look like I'm losing my grip so they have an excuse to push me out completely, and my husband just plays right into it, telling me I need help. But I don't need help, I just need them to stop messing with my life!\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing these things happening at work—the watching, the changing of numbers, the equipment being moved around?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and think about it, and trace it all back to when things started feeling... off, you know? It's been a while. It hasn't just been a couple of weeks or something that just popped up overnight. No, it's been going on for a solid eight months now. I remember because it was right around the time the weather started turning cold last year, right after Thanksgiving, when I first started getting this overwhelming feeling that eyes were on me. That's when the watching started. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the watching, either. That's when I first started hearing the voices, too. Not loud, not like someone yelling right in my ear, but just... murmurs. Whispers. Like people talking about me just out of earshot, saying things about how I wasn't doing my job right or how I was messing up. And then, right around that same time, maybe a week or two later, that's when the numbers started getting weird. I'd finish a shift, totally exhausted, knowing I'd hit my quota, and then the next day, the numbers would be down. And the equipment moving—that started a little later, maybe around January or February, when they realized just changing the numbers wasn't enough to break me. \n\nSo yeah, eight months of this. Eight solid months of looking over my shoulder, checking and re-checking my pallet counts, trying to figure out who's whispering, who's moving my jack. Eight months of coming home and trying to explain to my husband that my days are a constant battle, and him just... not getting it. He thinks I'm just tired from the physical labor, but it's not the lifting that wears you down, it's the constant, non-stop feeling of being targeted, day in and day out, for eight whole months. It's exhausting, it really is, and nobody down at the warehouse seems to care, and my husband just wants to sweep it under the rug. But I know what's been happening since last fall, I know it.\n\nDoctor: When you hear these whispers about you, can you make out specific words or phrases that they're saying, or do you just hear the sound of voices talking without being able to understand what they're actually saying?\n\nPatient: Well, sometimes it is just the murmuring, like I said, just that low buzzing sound of people talking just out of earshot where you can't quite catch it. But... no, a lot of times it is actual words. Like, very specific words. It's a man's voice, and it comes through clear as day. I'll be right in the middle of the aisle at work, maybe down in aisle four where we keep the heavy liquid detergents, the ones that always leak and leave that sticky mess everywhere on the racks, and I'll be standing there with my scanner, and I hear this man say, Nicole, check it again. Just like that. Clear as a bell. And I'll spin around, thinking maybe Mr. Henderson or one of the shift leads sneaked up on me to try and catch me messing up, but there is absolutely nobody in the bay. The whole aisle is totally empty. \n\nAnd the thing that really gets me, the thing that makes my husband just roll his eyes when I try to explain it to him, is that it doesn't even just happen at the warehouse anymore. I'll be at home, in my own house, just trying to get some chores done on my day off. I'll be standing in the living room folding clothes, you know, trying to get the collars flat on my husband's shirts, the TV isn't even on, the house is dead quiet, and I hear that exact same man's voice right over my shoulder saying, Nicole, check it again. And there is nobody near me! My husband might be out in the garage or at the store, and I'm completely alone in the house, but the voice is right there. \n\nSo it's not just me mishearing the forklift beeps or the warehouse echo, because why would I hear it in my own living room while I'm folding towels? It's like whoever is doing this to me, whoever is watching me and trying to ruin my numbers, they've found a way to follow me right into my own home. I don't know how they're doing it, but I hear exactly what they're saying, and it's exhausting because I'm constantly checking over my shoulder, checking my work, checking the doors, and nobody is ever standing there.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, or even before that, have there been times when you felt unusually energetic, like you needed much less sleep than normal but still felt rested and ready to go, or when your thoughts were racing so fast you could barely keep up with them?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you mention it... yeah. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because my husband and I were just arguing about my sleep schedule the other day. He's one of those people who absolutely needs his full eight hours, right? He's out like a light by 10 PM, just snoring away. But me? There have been these stretches, like whole weeks at a time, where I'll sleep maybe three hours, tops. \n\nI'll go to bed, and then my eyes just pop open at, like, 2:00 or 3:00 in the morning. I'll lay there looking at the alarm clock—it's one of those bright red digital ones, the kind that casts this weird red glow across the whole bedroom—and I wake up way before it even has a chance to go off at 5:00 AM. And the thing is, I don't feel groggy at all. Normally, if someone only gets three hours of sleep, you'd think they'd be dragging, right? You'd think they'd be falling asleep standing up at the warehouse. But not me. \n\nDuring those weeks, I wake up and I feel completely rested. I feel like I don't need any more rest to get moving, like my battery is fully charged and I'm just ready to go. I can't just lay there in the dark, so I'll get right up, start doing laundry, pack my lunch, maybe pull everything out of the pantry and reorganize the shelves. My mind is just going and going, thinking about the warehouse, thinking about how I'm going to stack the pallets that day, figuring out my whole routine before the sun is even up. My husband will come out to the kitchen rubbing his eyes, asking what on earth I'm doing scrubbing the counters at 3:30 in the morning, and I try to tell him I'm just awake and ready for the day! \n\nI actually feel amazing when that happens. I get to work and I'm practically vibrating, like I can out-work every single guy on the loading dock. I'm wrapping skids twice as fast as Greg or Mark. But of course, my husband just thinks it's another thing to complain about, telling me it's not normal to be up vacuuming before dawn. But yeah, during those weeks, three hours of sleep is all I need, and I'm totally good to go.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you're sleeping only three hours but feeling amazing and your mind is going and going, do you find yourself talking a lot more than usual, like people are telling you that you won't stop talking or that you're talking over them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... I mean, if I really think about it, no, I haven't noticed that at all. And nobody has said anything to me about it, either. You'd think my husband would be the very first one to complain if I was talking his ear off, right? Because, like I said, he complains about literally everything else—the vacuuming, the laundry at 3 AM, me trying to explain the situation with my pallet counts. But he's never once told me to stop talking or that I'm talking over him. We don't really even talk that much when I get in that zone, to be honest, because I'm just so focused on getting my chores done, getting the house perfect before I have to clock in. \n\nAnd then at work... well, the warehouse isn't exactly the kind of place where you can just stand around and have a conversation anyway. It's incredibly loud down there. You've got the backup alarms on the forklifts—that constant, piercing beep, beep, beep all shift long—and the big metal bay doors rattling every time an eighteen-wheeler backs in, and the conveyor belts just grinding away. Most of us wear those little squishy foam earplugs, the bright orange ones they keep in a huge plastic dispenser right by the time clock, so you practically have to scream just to ask someone to hand you a tape gun or a new roll of shrink wrap. \n\nSo during those weeks when I'm feeling really energized and my mind is just racing with all the ways I need to stack and sort the heavy freight, I'm definitely not wasting my time chatting with Greg or Mark or anybody else. I just keep my head down, wrap my skids, and keep moving. I'm just doing the work, you know? I'm not standing around running my mouth. So, no, I don't think I talk more than usual. I'm just getting things done.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you're feeling really energized and your mind is racing, have you ever felt like you had special abilities or insights that other people don't have, or like you were especially talented or important in some way?\n\nPatient: Special abilities? Like... what, like a superhero or something? Or like I'm some kind of secret genius? No, no, definitely not. I mean... I know I'm really good at my job when I get in that zone, like I was saying before. I can take a pallet of those heavy liquid detergents—the ones that are always a sticky mess—and I can stack them perfectly, interlocking the boxes so the weight is distributed just right, and then get the shrink wrap around the bottom so tight it won't ever budge during shipping. I can do all that way faster than Greg or Mark, but that's not a special ability, you know? That's just... that's just muscle memory and having a lot of energy. \n\nIt's not like I think I'm the CEO of the distribution center or that I have some secret master plan to run the whole company. I'm just a regular person trying to get my work done. If I was really that important, or if I had some kind of special talent, I wouldn't be spending my days dodging forklifts on a dirty concrete floor for an hourly wage, right? I'd be up in the air-conditioned office with Mr. Henderson, sitting in one of those nice rolling mesh chairs instead of wearing out the soles of my steel-toed boots. \n\nMy husband would definitely laugh if he heard you ask that. He's always reminding me that it's just a warehouse job and I shouldn't let it take over my life, especially when I'm up at 3 AM reorganizing the pantry. So, no, I don't feel like I have any secret insights or anything like that. I'm just me. I just happen to be really, really awake and focused sometimes, and I use that to get my numbers up and keep the house clean. There's nothing magical or special about it, I'm just... I just work hard when I have that energy.\n\nDoctor: Besides these weeks when you're feeling really energized on just three hours of sleep, have there been other stretches of time—maybe weeks or months—when you felt the opposite: really down, depressed, or like everything was hopeless, even if nothing bad had actually happened?\n\nPatient: Down? Like, depressed and crying and all that? No... no, I can't really say I've ever had a stretch like that. I mean, don't get me wrong, I get annoyed sometimes, like if Greg leaves a huge mess of broken shrink wrap and splintered wood in the aisle right when my shift starts, or if my husband is nagging me about the grocery bill again. But that's just normal life stuff, you know? It's not like I'm feeling hopeless or anything. \n\nI just don't really get that whole... sitting around in the dark feeling sorry for yourself thing. I honestly don't even have the time for it. I get up, I make my coffee—just regular Folgers in the drip machine, nothing fancy—I pack my lunch, usually just a turkey sandwich and some chips, and I go punch in at the warehouse. And after eight, sometimes ten hours on the concrete floor, lifting boxes of detergent that weigh half as much as I do, I come home, make dinner, watch maybe an hour of TV, and go to sleep. \n\nMy husband, he's always saying I'm like a machine, just go, go, go. He thinks I work way too hard for someone who didn't even finish high school, like I have something to prove to Mr. Henderson or the other guys on the dock or whatever. He'll say, \"You know you can just sit on the couch and do nothing for one night, right?\" But I don't like doing nothing. I like paying my bills. I like knowing the rent is covered and the pantry is stocked. So, yeah, hopelessness? Or feeling really down for months at a time? Not really. If I'm not bouncing off the walls with energy, I'm just doing my normal everyday routine. I don't really do the whole depression thing. I just keep moving, you know? There's always laundry to do or a shift to work, so I just focus on that.\n\nDoctor: Going back even further than the eight months at the warehouse, before all of this started—say, a year or two ago or even longer—have you ever had a period that lasted weeks or months where you felt really sad, empty, or like nothing was enjoyable anymore, even things you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: A year or two ago? Like, before the warehouse? No, honestly, it's just like I was saying before. I've never really been the type to get all sad and empty. \n\nLike... I mean, before I got hired on at the distribution center—this was probably, oh, maybe three or four years ago now—I was working at the Dollar General over on Route 9. You know the one, right next to where that old car wash used to be before they finally tore it down? Anyway, I was doing stock there, unloading those huge metal rolltainers they wheel off the trucks. I like watching my shows in the evening—I've watched Wheel of Fortune pretty much every night since I was a teenager. I never stopped liking that, never had a time where I just stared at the TV and didn't care. I didn't get down in the dumps about it. I just picked up extra shifts whenever somebody called out sick. \n\nMy husband complains about my schedule now, but he complained back then too, saying I was always tired from hauling boxes of bleach and seasonal decorations around all day. But being tired from working on your feet for hours isn't the same as being sad, you know? It's just physical exhaustion. Your feet ache, your back is stiff, so you just take a hot shower, eat your dinner, and go to bed. It's not this big emotional crisis. So, no, even going way back, I've never had a time where I just felt hopeless or empty for weeks on end. That's just not how I'm wired. I've always just put my head down and worked.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that man's voice saying 'Nicole, check it again,' does it feel like he's giving you a command or instruction that you feel compelled to follow, or is it more like you're just hearing words being spoken without any sense that you need to do what the voice is telling you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... Patient presents with clear bipolar I disorder with psychotic features: (1) Distinct manic episodes with decreased need for sleep (3 hours, feeling rested), racing thoughts, increased goal-directed activity, and increased productivity; (2) Prominent delusions of reference and persecution (coworkers sabotaging her, watching her) lasting 8 months; (3) Command hallucinations (hearing male voice saying 'Nicole, check it again') occurring both at work and at home, which she experiences as external and compelling; (4) Absence of sustained depressive episodes; (5) Preserved insight into some areas (denies grandiosity, acknowledges just working hard). I'll be standing in the living room folding clothes, you know, trying to get the collars flat on my husband's shirts, the TV isn't even on, the house is dead quiet, and I hear that exact same man's voice right over my shoulder saying, Nicole, check it again. And there is nobody near me! My husband might be out in the garage or at the store, and I'm completely alone in the house, but the voice is right there. \n\nSo it's not just me mishearing the forklift beeps or the warehouse echo, because why would I hear it in my own living room while I'm folding towels? It's like whoever is doing this to me, whoever is watching me and trying to ruin my numbers, they've found a way to follow me right into my own home. I don't know how they're doing it, but I hear exactly what they're saying, and it's exhausting because I'm constantly checking over my shoulder, checking my work, checking the doors, and nobody is ever standing there.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, or even before that, have there been times when you felt unusually energetic, like you needed much less sleep than normal but still felt rested and ready to go, or when your thoughts were racing so fast you could barely keep up with them?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you mention it... yeah. It's actually kind of funny you ask that, because my husband and I were just arguing about my sleep schedule the other day. He's one of those people who absolutely needs his full eight hours, right? He's out like a light by 10 PM, just snoring away. But me? There have been these stretches, like whole weeks at a time, where I'll sleep maybe three hours, tops. \n\nI'll go to bed, and then my eyes just pop open at, like, 2:00 or 3:00 in the morning. I'll lay there looking at the alarm clock—it's one of those bright red digital ones, the kind that casts this weird red glow across the whole bedroom—and I wake up way before it even has a chance to go off at 5:00 AM. And the thing is, I don't feel groggy at all. Normally, if someone only gets three hours of sleep, you'd think they'd be dragging, right? You'd think they'd be falling asleep standing up at the warehouse. But not me. \n\nDuring those weeks, I wake up and I feel completely rested. I feel like I don't need any more rest to get moving, like my battery is fully charged and I'm just ready to go. I can't just lay there in the dark, so I'll get right up, start doing laundry, pack my lunch, maybe pull everything out of the pantry and reorganize the shelves. My mind is just going and going, thinking about the warehouse, thinking about how I'm going to stack the pallets that day, figuring out my whole routine before the sun is even up. My husband will come out to the kitchen rubbing his eyes, asking what on earth I'm doing scrubbing the counters at 3:30 in the morning, and I try to tell him I'm just awake and ready for the day! \n\nI actually feel amazing when that happens. I get to work and I'm practically vibrating, like I can out-work every single guy on the loading dock. I'm wrapping skids twice as fast as Greg or Mark. But of course, my husband just thinks it's another thing to complain about, telling me it's not normal to be up vacuuming before dawn. But yeah, during those weeks, three hours of sleep is all I need, and I'm totally good to go.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you're sleeping only three hours but feeling amazing and your mind is going and going, do you find yourself talking a lot more than usual, like people are telling you that you won't stop talking or that you're talking over them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... I mean, if I really think about it, no, I haven't noticed that at all. And nobody has said anything to me about it, either. You'd think my husband would be the very first one to complain if I was talking his ear off, right? Because, like I said, he complains about literally everything else—the vacuuming, the laundry at 3 AM, me trying to explain the situation with my pallet counts. But he's never once told me to stop talking or that I'm talking over him. We don't really even talk that much when I get in that zone, to be honest, because I'm just so focused on getting my chores done, getting the house perfect before I have to clock in. \n\nAnd then at work... well, the warehouse isn't exactly the kind of place where you can just stand around and have a conversation anyway. It's incredibly loud down there. You've got the backup alarms on the forklifts—that constant, piercing beep, beep, beep all shift long—and the big metal bay doors rattling every time an eighteen-wheeler backs in, and the conveyor belts just grinding away. Most of us wear those little squishy foam earplugs, the bright orange ones they keep in a huge plastic dispenser right by the time clock, so you practically have to scream just to ask someone to hand you a tape gun or a new roll of shrink wrap. \n\nSo during those weeks when I'm feeling really energized and my mind is just racing with all the ways I need to stack and sort the heavy freight, I'm definitely not wasting my time chatting with Greg or Mark or anybody else. I just keep my head down, wrap my skids, and keep moving. I'm just doing the work, you know? I'm not standing around running my mouth. So, no, I don't think I talk more than usual. I'm just getting things done.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you're feeling really energized and your mind is racing, have you ever felt like you had special abilities or insights that other people don't have, or like you were especially talented or important in some way?\n\nPatient: Special abilities? Like... what, like a superhero or something? Or like I'm some kind of secret genius? No, no, definitely not. I mean... I know I'm really good at my job when I get in that zone, like I was saying before. I can take a pallet of those heavy liquid detergents—the ones that are always a sticky mess—and I can stack them perfectly, interlocking the boxes so the weight is distributed just right, and then get the shrink wrap around the bottom so tight it won't ever budge during shipping. I can do all that way faster than Greg or Mark, but that's not a special ability, you know? That's just... that's just muscle memory and having a lot of energy. \n\nIt's not like I think I'm the CEO of the distribution center or that I have some secret master plan to run the whole company. I'm just a regular person trying to get my work done. If I was really that important, or if I had some kind of special talent, I wouldn't be spending my days dodging forklifts on a dirty concrete floor for an hourly wage, right? I'd be up in the air-conditioned office with Mr. Henderson, sitting in one of those nice rolling mesh chairs instead of wearing out the soles of my steel-toed boots. \n\nMy husband would definitely laugh if he heard you ask that. He's always reminding me that it's just a warehouse job and I shouldn't let it take over my life, especially when I'm up at 3 AM reorganizing the pantry. So, no, I don't feel like I have any secret insights or anything like that. I'm just me. I just happen to be really, really awake and focused sometimes, and I use that to get my numbers up and keep the house clean. There's nothing magical or special about it, I'm just... I just work hard when I have that energy.\n\nDoctor: Besides these weeks when you're feeling really energized on just three hours of sleep, have there been other stretches of time—maybe weeks or months—when you felt the opposite: really down, depressed, or like everything was hopeless, even if nothing bad had actually happened?\n\nPatient: Down? Like, depressed and crying and all that? No... no, I can't really say I've ever had a stretch like that. I mean, don't get me wrong, I get annoyed sometimes, like if Greg leaves a huge mess of broken shrink wrap and splintered wood in the aisle right when my shift starts, or if my husband is nagging me about the grocery bill again. But that's just normal life stuff, you know? It's not like I'm feeling hopeless or anything. \n\nI just don't really get that whole... sitting around in the dark feeling sorry for yourself thing. I honestly don't even have the time for it. Even when I'm not having one of those crazy energetic weeks where I'm up at 3 AM scrubbing the baseboards and running on three hours of sleep, I'm still just doing my regular routine. I get up, I make my coffee—just regular Folgers in the drip machine, nothing fancy—I pack my lunch, usually just a turkey sandwich and some chips, and I go punch in at the warehouse. And after eight, sometimes ten hours on the concrete floor, lifting boxes of detergent that weigh half as much as I do, I come home, make dinner, watch maybe an hour of TV, and go to sleep. \n\nMy husband, he's always saying I'm like a machine, just go, go, go. He thinks I work way too hard for someone who didn't even finish high school, like I have something to prove to Mr. Henderson or the other guys on the dock or whatever. He'll say, \"You know you can just sit on the couch and do nothing for one night, right?\" But I don't like doing nothing. I like paying my bills. I like knowing the rent is covered and the pantry is stocked. So, yeah, hopelessness? Or feeling really down for months at a time? Not really. If I'm not bouncing off the walls with energy, I'm just doing my normal everyday routine. I don't really do the whole depression thing. I just keep moving, you know? There's always laundry to do or a shift to work, so I just focus on that.\n\nDoctor: Going back even further than the eight months at the warehouse, before all of this started—say, a year or two ago or even longer—have you ever had a period that lasted weeks or months where you felt really sad, empty, or like nothing was enjoyable anymore, even things you normally like to do?\n\nPatient: A year or two ago? Like, before the warehouse? No, honestly, it's just like I was saying before. I've never really been the type to get all sad and empty. \n\nLike... I mean, before I got hired on at the distribution center—this was probably, oh, maybe three or four years ago now—I was working at the Dollar General over on Route 9. You know the one, right next to where that old car wash used to be before they finally tore it down? Anyway, I was doing stock there, unloading those huge metal rolltainers they wheel off the trucks. And let me tell you, those things are an absolute nightmare if the warehouse didn't pack them right, you've got stuff just falling everywhere, heavy cans of soup and huge bags of dog food just waiting to crush your foot. But even then, dealing with all that mess for way less pay, I wasn't... I wasn't depressed or anything. I was just working. \n\nMy manager back then, Brenda, she used to have me on the opening shift a lot, so I'd be there at 6 AM, unlocking the glass doors, getting the registers ready with the tills, pulling all the broken down cardboard the night crew was supposed to take out but never did. I didn't have the time to sit around feeling empty. \n\nAnd as far as not enjoying things... I mean, what's there to stop enjoying? I like keeping my house clean. I like watching my shows in the evening—I've watched Wheel of Fortune pretty much every night since I was a teenager. I never stopped liking that, never had a time where I just stared at the TV and didn't care. Even when money was a lot tighter, before I got this warehouse job, because the dollar store only paid minimum wage and Brenda barely gave me thirty hours a week so she wouldn't have to give me benefits... I didn't get down in the dumps about it. I just picked up extra shifts whenever somebody called out sick. \n\nMy husband complains about my schedule now, but he complained back then too, saying I was always tired from hauling boxes of bleach and seasonal decorations around all day. But being tired from working on your feet for hours isn't the same as being sad, you know? It's just physical exhaustion. Your feet ache, your back is stiff, so you just take a hot shower, eat your dinner, and go to bed. It's not this big emotional crisis. So, no, even going way back, I've never had a time where I just felt hopeless or empty for weeks on end. That's just not how I'm wired. I've always just put my head down and worked.\n\nDoctor: When you hear that man's voice saying 'Nicole, check it again,' does it feel like he's giving you a command or instruction that you feel compelled to follow, or is it more like you're just hearing words being spoken without any sense that you need to do what the voice is telling you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I mean, I usually do check whatever I'm doing, because it sounds so real. It's not like I think it's some magic spell forcing me to do it or anything like that, it's just... it's so clear. Clear as day. Sometimes when I'm in the aisle at work—like down in the shipping section where it's usually super loud with the forklifts backing up and the warning beepers going off—I'll just be wrapping a pallet or checking a packing slip and I hear this man's voice right over my shoulder. \"Nicole, check it again.\" \n\nAnd I always turn around, thinking it's Greg messing with me, or maybe the shift supervisor coming up behind me to check my tags, but there's nobody there. Nobody is near me at all, just empty concrete and racks of boxes. \n\nAnd it's not even just at the warehouse where it's busy and there's people around. It happens when it's dead quiet, too. Like, I'll be at home folding clothes in the living room. Just standing there sorting my husband's work shirts—the heavy denim ones that I always have to double-check the collars on for grease stains before I put them on the hangers—and matching up all his black socks, and I'll hear the exact same man's voice. \"Nicole, check it again.\" Clear as day, right in my own house. \n\nSo, yeah, I guess I do follow the instruction, because I'll stop and re-check the collar on the shirt, or I'll recount the pairs of socks in the basket, because the voice is just so... present, you know? It's not a whisper. It doesn't sound like it's in my head. It's just a guy telling me to check my work. So I do. I mean, if someone was standing right next to you and told you to check something, you'd probably look at it again too, right? But I'll look around the living room, and my husband is out in the driveway working on his truck, the TV isn't even on yet, and I'm completely alone. But I still hear it. I just check the clothes again and keep folding."
  }
